[trialAccount t19070910-102a]
FRIEND, John (34, labourer), and LUCK, Thomas (42, baker and confectioner); both stealing a mare, the goods of Frank William Brown, and feloniously receiving same; both stealing a cart, the goods of John Dunn Halliday, and feloniously receiving same; both stealing a pony, a cart, and two sets of harness, the goods of Frederick Henderson, and feloniously receiving same; both stealing a gelding, the goods of Richard James Keen, and feloniously receiving same.
Mr. J. Wells Thatcher and Mr. P. B. Petrides prosecuted; Mr. Forrest Fulton defended Luck.
FRANK WILLIAM BROWN , Folly Farm, Folly Lane, Chingford. I had a bay mare at grass on July 11 in a field opposite my house. On the morning of the 12th, at 4.30, the mare was not there. It was daylight then. I hunted all round the field, and not finding it reported it missing. A chestnut gelding belonging to Mr. Richard Keen was also missing. On August 3 I went to Dorking, where I saw the chestnut gelding in Luck's van. Luck himself was driving the horse. I waited about two hours to see if I could see the other one. I afterwards spoke to Luck as he was coming out of a police-house, and asked him where the mare was. I said to him, "You had two horses down here on July 12?" He said, "Yes." I said, "I have seen one of the horses in your van this afternoon. Can you tell me where the other one is?" He wanted to know who I was and I told him that the two horses which came down on the 12th were stolen, and I was the owner. After I had satisfied him as to who I was he told me he had sold it to a baker company. I asked him if it was anywhere in the town, and he said, "No; it is at Bromley, in Kent." He told me he had sold the mare for £35, and that a man named Smith had sold the animals to him. He said he did not know Smith's address just then, but he could find him, and that he had given £9 for the mare and gelding and two ponies in exchange. On August 4 I went to Bromley with Detective-sergeant Phipps and Mr. Keen and identified the mare. I had no doubt about her whatever. After that we went to Dorking, where Mr. Keen identified his horse.
Cross-examined. Reen's was a striking-looking horse, easily recognisable. When I first saw it at Dorking it was being driven in a baker's van with Luck's name upon it. The police had ascertained that two horses were consigned from London Bridge to Dorking on July 12. Luck made no concealment about it. The first I knew of the whereabouts of my horse was from information given me by Luck himself. I do not suggest that he refused to give me Smith's address. Luck offered to go to the police station with me. There was nothing the matter with the horse when I lost it.
RICHARD JAMES KEEN , Cherry Farm, Chingford. I put the golding out to grass on July 10, and it was missing on the morning the 12th. I next saw it on August 4 at Luck's stables. I value it at £35.
EDWARD GODLONTON , 40, Coleridge road, Walthamstone, meter tester, employed by the Gas Light and Coke Company. I have sons allotment ground near Folly Lane, but I cannot see Mr. Brown's field from those allotments, as it runs rather low there. On the evening of July 11 I was engaged on my allotment from half past six till nine. Three men passed who struck my attention, because every time I looked to they were looking towards me, and they bring strangers I thought it rather suspicions. They were going towards Mr. Brown's farm. One was carrying a braided affair which looked very much like a halter. I watched them till they were out or sight. On the morning of the 14th Mr. Brown came and told me of his loss. On August 9, at Stoke Newington Station, out of about 20 men, I picked out Friend as one of the men I saw pass my ground I have no doubt he is the man.
ALBERT ETHERTON , 15, Russell Road, Dorking, railway porter. I remember two horses arriving at Dorking Station in the middle of July, at 11.53 a.m., consigned from London Bridge by train leaving at 10.38. Luck, who had been waiting in front of the station, paid for their carriage. I saw prisoner Friend there. He asked me where Mr. Luck was as I was uncoupling the horse box. I told him he had gone into the booking office to sign for the horses, and as I looked up I saw him coming down the platform. Luck and Friend led the horses away. On August 8 I identified Friend at Dorking Police Station.
Cross-examined. I have been at Dorking Station about 14 years and have known Luck for a good many years as an honest, straight-forward tradesman. I know he keeps three or four horses and west, which he uses is his business as a master baker.
LOUIS WILLIAM FOOKS , Cat for, managing director of the Brosley Co-operative Society. I first met Luck in January last in connection with buying a horse which he advertised for sale. On July 19 I received a letter from him offering "A good, useful vanner, quiet in every way, six years old. If your committee is looking for a good, genuine animal they will do well to see this. Price 35 guiness." The matter was placed before the committee of my society, and I was deputed, in conjunction with our horsekeeper, to go and see this horse, and we went to Luck's residence in Pixham Lane. He asked 60 as for two horses, and ultimately I decided to give him £35 for the brown mare and offered him £25 for the gelding. I made out a receipt, at Mr. Luck's request, as follows: "Received of Mr. L. W. Fooks the sum of £3 as part payment of bay mare for the Bromley Co-operative Society." I stamped it and got Luck to sign it. Subsequently he was notified to send the other horse on for trial for a few weeks, but he informed us that he had loaned it to his Geomenry officer for a fornight. It did not strike me to ask Luck where he got either of these horses. I was at his place early in January last, and I said to him, "Our society is frequently in the habit of buying horses of good character, and any time you know of a good one let us know." He said his brother was a farmer, that he bred horses,
and brought them in for sale, so we presumed when we received this letter it might have been a horse which his brother had broken in. After I had bought the horse I said to Luck, "Is this from your brother's farm?" and he said "Yes."
Cross-examined. I did not mention about the brother's farm at the police court, as the clerk only gave me set questions. I only recollected it yesterday. The detective asked me whether Luck had told me that he had a brother who was a farmer. When Luck was at Faltham Cross he asked me whether I could become bail for him for £100. I said "No; I won't become surety for you. Your brother is a farmer, why not appeal to him?"
Detective-sergeant WILLIAM PHIPPS , Walthamstow. On July 12 I received information concerning this case. In company with Keen and Brown, I went to the Bromley Co-operative Society's stables on August 4, where Brown identified the bay mare. We then went to Dorking, where I met the prisoner Luck in the WaLtham, a small opening in the Dorking High Road. I told him I was a police officer and was making inquiries about two horses stolen from Chingford on July 10, one of which he had sold to the Co-operative Society for £35. I said I believed he had the other one in his possession. He said "Yes" and invited me to his home in Pixham Lane. In the stablewe saw the bay gelding. After I had cautioned him he said, "A man named Smith brought the horses to me at Dorking Station. I bought them in exchange for £9 and two ponies. Smith, I think, lives at Hardinge Street, Commercial Road. I had seen him about three or you months previous to this. I had no receipt for the horses. I ought they were straight." I said, "How do you account for the orses being consigned to you a few hours after they were stolen?" He made no reply. I then told him I should arrest him for being concerned in stealing and receiving these horses. He replied, "Smith sent me a wire saying they were coming. They got here about midday. I did not know they were crooked (stolen)." I conveyed him to Dorking Police Station, where he was detained all night. Next morning while waiting for a train at Dorking he said, "I saw Smith last Sunday. I have been to his house. I will tell you about it. Smith lives at 15, Drewton Street, Hardinge Street." As a matter of fact, Smith does not live there, but his sister or sister-in-law does. On the 9th I was present at the Stoke Newington Police Station, when Friend was put up for identification with several other men and was immediately picked out by the witness Godlonton without any hesitation. On the 13th, at Watham, Friend was confronted with Luck, and I read to Friend the statements made by Luck. Friend replied, "I am as innocent as Mr. Luck. I did not know the horses were stolen. They were fetched round to me. I went for some butter, and when returning from the shop a man Wado had the horses. I do not know where Wado lives. I asked him if he was doing to the Barbican. He said, "It does not matter. I have the privilege to sell them." I have known Wado three or four months, but have not had much conversation with him. I asked him what
the horses were worth. He said, "They were allowed out to me to take £30 home for them. I put them on the rail, Mr. Luck saw me give the money to another man, who gave me £1." Friend was then charged with Luck and made no further reply. I have made inquiries concerning Wado, but can hear nothing of him.
Cross-examined. It was owing to the action of Luck's son that I was able to arrest Friend. Friend came to Luck's house, the seperintendent of police was sent for, and he was arrested at the house. At the station Luck identified Friend as the man Smith he had been speaking of. Friend admitted he was Smith, and that Luck's statements were true.
Detective-sergeant ALBERT HAWKINS , Stoke Newington. I was present when the prisoner Friend and two other men were discharged by the Justices at Dorking Police Court on August 9. I told Friend I was going to arrest him and take him back to London for stealing two horses from Chingford on July 12. He said, "I never stole from Chingford, I was at my house getting a cup of tea for my missing at eight o'clock on the morning of July 12. She was expecting to go to bed (to be confined). I live at 542, Old Ford Road, and went out to get some buter, and when I came back with it I not a man named Wado. He had two horses with him, and I asked him if he was going to the Barbican. He asked me if I knew anyone who would buy them, and I said I knew several people. I asked him how much he wanted for them, and he said £30 for the two. I told him if he liked he could go with me and put them on the rail at London Bridge. I knew a man named Luck, who would buy them. I took the buter home and then went with Wado. I sent a wire to Luck to meet me at Dorking Station, and Mr. Luck was there and met us. Wado led the horses to Mr. Luck's place, and I jumped up with Mr. Luck in his trap. The horses were put in the van and tried, and Mr. Luck gave me £8 10s. in gold and two ponies, which were put on the rail for London Bridge, and Wado took them away. I got a sovereign out of it for my trouble; Wado gave it to me." From Old Ford Road to London Bridge Station in about four miles. I should think it would be possible to get from Old Ford to London Bridge by the time the telegram was despatched (8.50 a.m.).
FRANK HOLLAMBY , of the General Post Office, produced the original of the telegram dispatched from London Bridge by Friend.
CHARLES STEVENS , booking clerk, Dorking, proved the arrival of the two horses by the 11.53 train, and that they were signed for by prisoner Luck, who paid the charges, 13s.
JOHN FRIEND (prisoner on oath). At the request of my wife on July 12 I got up and went across the road facing my house to get some butter. As I returned from the shop I met a man who is called Wado in the Old Ford Road. I asked him, "Are you going to the Barbican?" Friday is the sale day at the Repository. He replied, "Yes. Let me know anybody that will buy them. I am privileged to sell them." I asked him how much he had to take back to the owner, and he said £30. I told him a man named Luck, of Dorking,
would buy them, and I said I would take them down and let him have a look at them. Wado led the horses to the station, which took, I think, about three-quarters of an hour, and they were put in a truck and sent to Dorking. I sent a telegram to Luck saying that the horses were coming and met him at Dorking Station. Luck asked the price and I told him £30. "Well," he said, "they look cheap horses. Are they all right?" I replied, "As far as I know." They were led round to Luck's place in Pixham Lane and tried in the van. Luck gave me £9 for them and two ponies, which I handed over to Wado. I thought the horses were straightforward and not stolen horses. As to the witness Goldton saying that he saw me at Chingford on July 11, I can assure you I was on the flat of my back on the 10th and 11th, and only got up on July 12, I am perfectly innocent of having stolen the horses.
Cross-examined. Wado has been up and down Old Ford for about three months as near as I can remember. If I said I did not know saying about horses I should be a liar. I know a little. I asked wado where he got the horses form and he told me he was working for his governor. I know Chingford, but I was not there on the 11th as Godlonton said. I was suffering from a ricked neck and horrible pains in the head and had been in and out of London infirmary. I was, however, well enough to take a trip to Dorking on the 12th. The horses were led along burdett Road, Stepney, into Cablee Street and over Tower Bridge into Tooley Street. We had no trouble in getting over the bridge. I have seen wado once since when he told me he had sold the two ponies, one for £15 and one for £7. I have not tried to find him. I thought they were straight forwared horses.
The following evidence was given with reference to other stolen property traced to Luck's possession.
FREDERICK HENDERSON , florist, 694, High Road, Tottenham. On March 21, I missed a pony and trap and two sets of harness from my stables in Reform Road. The trap, which was of mahogany colour, was found at "The Gun" beerhouse at Dorking, and restored to me by the detectives in August. I valued the pony and trap at £35.
WILLIAM LLEWELLYN SAUNDERS , "The Gun" beerhouse, North Street. Dorking. I purchased the pony and trap in question of the prisoner Luck for £16, a mare and two pigs. The mare was worth £7, and the two pigs I gave 14s. each for. I kept the trap till the detectives told me it was stolen property; Luck told me his wife was sorry he had sold the pony and trap, and he wanted to buy it back from me at a slight premium. I had, of course, no idea that the cart and pony were stolen. I took a fancy to the turnout, as I thought I could take my wife out with it.
Cross-examined. I have known Luck 11 years as a well-known master baker in Dorking. He has always borne the character of an honest and respectable man.
JOHN STEWART , 200, Silver Street, Edmonton, carman to Mr. Henderson, spoke to securing the pony and cart at half past eight at night, on March 21, with two padlocks, and to going with Mr. Henderson to identify the things on August 7.
JOHN DUNN HALLIDAY , draper, 214, Burdett Road, Bow. On March 31, I had a Stratford cart and some harness which was loss. A pony was lost at the same time from the stable. I next saw the trap at the Dorking Police Station on August 11. It was not in the same condition as when it was stolem. When I had it it was of a dark green colour, picked out with black, and the cushions were dark green. When I saw it at Dorking it was a dark blue with cushions to match. The name of the maker had been erased from the axle boxes and the splashboards altered. I knew it to be my trap by the tyres and one or two marks I could swear to.
Cross-examined. I valued the trap at £20. I did not see say of Mr. Luck's other carts, and could not say whether my cart was painted the same.
ALBERT WYATT SHERLOCK , Cypress Villas, Dorking, carriage builder. I have had business dealings with Thomas Luck. On May 10 his son brought me a Stratford cart, with instructions that it was to be thoroughly done up. The cart was taken to pieces, thoroughly cleaned, and painted blue and yellow, the shafts being yellow picked out in black, and the straight wings replaced with bent. It looked a totally different cart when I finished with it. The caps had some grit marks on them and these were removed with a file, and the name of the maker was also filed off. I do not remember what the name was. The cushions were re-covered with blue cloth. My charge was £5 10s., which Mr. Luck paid yesterday morning. He runs an account with me. The work was done in my shop in West Street, and a passer by could easily see the trap. After it was finished it was put in the show-room facing the street.
Cross-examined. Luck is a respectable and well-known tradesman in the town. I have done work for him for a number of years. I had three carts of his to overhaul in May and June. It is the usual practice to clean the caps of the axles with a file. the name is never very deeply indented, and any cleaning of the caps would obliterate it. Luck's trade colours are blue, yellow and, black. With yellow we always have blue as it suite the yellow. There is no truth in the statement that the cart was sent to me in order that I might disguise it. I put the value of the cart at about £8.
Superintendent GEORGE BOON , Surrey County Constabulary, stationed at Dorking. I had some information and made inquiries about a stolen Stratford cart, and on May 10 I saw Luck at the station, where he called to deliver bread to one of the constables living there. Luck said to me, "You are making inquiries about a horse, cart, and harness." I said I was. He said, "I bought a pony, trap, and harness from some dealers, which I sold to Saunders at 'The Gun.'" That is the turnout which has been indentified
as the property of Mr. Henderson. I said, "I have already seen that, but it does not correspond with what I am inquiring about at all. This Stratford cart that I am inquiring about is painted black, picked out with green, and has rubber tyres." Luck replied that the cart he sold to Saunders was the only cart he had had. At the time, I had no suspicion of Luck. Later on I found the Stratforad cart at Luck's premises, covered over with a white cloth. It was different in appearance form the description originally given to me, being blue and yellow instead of green and balck.
Cross-examined. The cover over the cart was the ordinary carriage cover to keep the dust off. The statement about the pony and cart sold to Saunders was quite voluntary.
Detective-sergeant HENRY DAVIS , stationed at Tottenham. On August 7 I went to "The Gun" beerhouse, where I saw a pony cart, and some harness which I recognised as belonging to Henderson.
Detective-sergeant WILLIAM PHIPPS , recalled. On August 13 I was at Waltham Abbey Petty Session Court. Luck was charged with stealing and receiving a pony and cart, the property of Mr. Henderson. He replied in the presence of Friday: "I bought it of a man I know as Tom Smith. There were others there." Friend was then charged, and replied, "I am innocent of it. I was there when the money was paid, but I was not with the other man."
Judge Lumley Smith observed that he would have to tell the jury that Henderson's cart did not come within the statute at all, and they would have to disregard it. Halliday's cart was another matter.
(Defence.)
THOMAS LUCK (prisoner, on oath). I have been in business in Pixham Lane, Dorking, for 18 years. I have never before this been in the hands of the police or been charged with any criminal offence. I employ two or three carts in my business. They are painted with blue bodies and yellow wheels. These are my regular colours. About the end of March I purchased the horse, trap, and harness, which was sold to Saunders at the "Ram Inn," Dorking, of a man called "Tom" for £16 10s., which in my judgment was a fair price. Saunders stopped me as I was driving past his place, and we had some conversation which led to the deal. A little later I heard rumours about the pony, cart and harness and had the conversation with the superintendent which he has related. I first met Friday (or Smith) on the Thursday before I bought the pony, cart, and harness with several other dealers. They were down for a periodical horse sale which is held twice a year, but had made a mistake in the date. The spring sale has generally taken palce on the Thursday before Good Friday, but it was a fortnight later this year. They had horses for sale. I bought a horse off one of the other dealers. About a fortnight or three weeks afterwards I met Smith in Dorking, and we went to the "Tuns" together. I exchanged a roan mare for the Stratford cart and another cob that he had. I
had no reason to believe that Smith was not an honest man or that the cart had not been properly come by. I had seen him in Dorking several times. On July 12 I received the telegram which has been produced by the Post Office that two horses were consigned to see at Dorking Station. I paid for the two horses as described, and they were taken round to my shop. I valued the two ponies I gave in exchange at £20, and that with £9 in gold makes £29, which I considered a fair price. The bay mare that I sold to the co-operative Society had a large knee, and that, in my opinion, reduces its value. In purchasing horses from dealers in the ordinary way I do not get any warranty; there is a certain amount of risk about such purchases. The Stratford cart was not sent to the carriage builders for its appearance to be altered. It is not correct to suggest that I ordered the maker's name to be obliterated from the wheels. I sent it to be thoroughly done up. The first time I had inquiries about the two horses was on the Saturday before the August Bank Holiday, when I saw Mr. Brown. Up to that time I had no suspicion that the horses had been stolen or dishonestly come by. I told the detective next evening how they had come into my possession. I had up till then only known Friend as J. Smith. I had Smith's address at Drewton Street, Commercial Road through him writing to me from there. When I saw Friend at the police-station I identified him as the man Smith from whom I had purchased the horses. He was arrested close to my shop. My son had him arrested. I purchased the animals in good faith.
Cross-examined. When I saw the horses they struck me as being very good ones. The mare improved wonderfully after I had her. It did not strike me as being a curious deal or anything out of the way. I have had altogether three transactions with Friend—a cob, a cart, and these two horses. The cob I sold to a man named Ford, who lives at Hedley. Friend had a standing order to bring me any decent animal he could get hold of. I have not done anything with horses until this year. I have not seen "Tom" since I bought Henderson's cart of him that I know of. You can call it a nice little lot; it seized me very well. My wife fancied it. "Tom" was known to Swift, who kept the "Three Tuns," where the periodical sales are held and I asked him about him and thought I had satisfied myself that he was all right. I purchased three carts within about a month. My instructions to Sherlock about the Stratford cart were that I wanted it done up properly. I told him the colours. I tried to sell the Stratford cart. Everything I have is for sale. After my son fetched it home it stood in an open shed and I chucked a sheet over it to keep the dust off.
BROMLEY HALL , master miller, Godstone, near Dorking; WILLIAM KING . blacksmith, Ross Road, Dorking; and JOSEPH HENRY SMITH . 303, Haydon Road, Wimbledon, were called as to Luck's character.
Verdict: Friend, Guilty; Luck, Not guilty, on the ground of insufficient evidence. For sentence on Friend see conclusion of next case.
(Wednesday, September 18.)
[trialAccount t19070910-103]
FRIEND, John (34, labourer), and RILEY, Henry (37, dealer) ; to the stealing a horse, the goods of Emma Munday and feloniously receiving same; both stealing one horse collar; one pair reins, and other articles, the goods of William Grant, and feloniously receiving same; both stealing one saddle one bridle, and other articles, the goods of Henry Harvery, and feloniously receiving same.
Mr. Thatcher prosecuted; Mr. Joyce Thomas defended Riley.
EMMA MUNDAY , Wife of Richard Munday, labourer, Clapton. On August Bank Holiday I had a bay horse at grass in Reader's field, close to Lea Bridge. It was safe at five o'clock in the evening. Early next morning, when I went to look for it to take it to Spitalfields Market, it was gone. I am a costermonger, and help to get my own living and the childern's. When next I saw it it was at Dorking on the 9th, and 18 inches had been cut off its tale. I know prisoner Riley. He came to me to buy some fowls and a set of harness, but I could not say for certain when that was. He did not buy the fowls as the price was too high. Riley saw the cob on two occasions and asked whether it was for sale. The value of the horse was £14. It had on, in the field, a leather head stall, which was found in the trap at Barking. There was also found in the cart a collar I had sold to Riley. The collar was too small to go over my horse's head. Later on I saw Riley at Stoke Newington Police Station, and charged him. I value the halter at a shilling. I was shown some of the harness which I did not recognise. I know nothing of the harness except the collar. There was a complete set and some head pieces. The harness was found at a livery bait stables, and I paid 7s. 6d. for the feed of the horse on 6th 7th 8th and 9th. When I saw the horse it was in the custody of the police.
To prisoner Friend. I have had the horse 16 years. I am not accustomed to let it out to anybody.
To Mr. Thomas. I do not exactly remember the date when I sold some harness to Riley.
Re-examined. If someone else has declared that this particular collar was theirs, very likely Mr. Riley sold it to him. It was mine once.
WILLIAM GRANT , 10, Ribstone Street, Hackney, fire-wood dealer Between August 5 and 6 I lost some harness. I identified it on the Thursday week following, at the Stoke Newington Police Station. I know Riley, but I cannot say whether he know where my stable is. A man named Harvey uses the same stable as I do, and I sometimes have lent the harness to him. Harvey sometimes lent me his harness. There was no lock on the stable, only a but outside. This is the first time I have loss anything from the stable.
HENRY HARVERY , Brooksby's Walk, Homerton. I missed some harness on the Tuesday after Bank Holiday, from the stable I jointly occupy with Grant. I identified the harness at Stoke Newington Police Station. I know Riley, to whom I have sold two horses.
(Asked to identify his harness from that produced, witness identified a colar, Thames, traces, pad, and breeching; also a bridle to which there was no bit.)
To Mr. Thomas. I exchanged a collar with a Mr. Gollidge. I have had it for five or six months. I do not know whether it is the one Mrs. Munday identified. I did not see her.
Detective-sergeant ALBERT HAWKINS , N Division. On August 9 I saw the prisoners, Friend and Riley, at Dorking Police Court, and said to them, "I am going to arrest you both for stealing a cob and harness, which has been found in your possession." Riley said, "I never got in the trap. I do not know anything about it. I only went with Friend for a drive." Friend said, "I wish to make a statement. I borrowed the horse from a man named J. Lovell, on Monday night, in the Lea Bridge Road. Lovell lives at Bethnal Green, but I do not know where. Lovell had the cob which had the set of harnes on, which was on the animal when I was arrested at Dorking. He had a rug over the horse, and was carrying the saddle under his arm. I said, 'Hallos! what is the matter?' He said 'My trap has broken down, and I have left it up the road.' I not Riley shortly afterwards in the 'Ship Aground' public-house in Lee Bridge Road, and asked him if he knew anyone who had a trust Riley said his landlord would lend him one for a bit of silver. I saw Lovell standing outside with the cob, harness, and cart. He took it away. Next morning (Tuesday, August 6), he came round about 9 a.m. and asked if I knew anyone who would buy a cob. I said, 'Yes,' and took the cob down to Mr. Luck at Dorking, and sold it to him. Lovell left me, and I have not seen him since. On the way to Dorking, I met Riley and Gardner in the Old Ford Road, and took them for a drive. I made inquiries about the trap, and found it belonged to a man named Head, Riley's landlord."
To Mr. Thomas. When I first saw Head he said he did not lend it, but when it was a question of coming to police court to charge Riley with stealing the cart he said he remembered that he had lent it to him.
Superintendent GEORGE BOON , Surrey Constabulary. At 4.45 p.m. on August 6 I saw prisoner Friend in Pixham Lane, Dorking. He was driving a bay gelding in an old two-wheel cart. I stopped him and asked him if his name was Jack smith. He replied, "No, John Friend." I told him I was going to arrest him on another charge and conveyed him to Dorking Police Station. Ascertaining that two men had been in his company, I went out and found them, prisoner Riley being one and the other a man named Gardner. They were also taken to Dorking Police Station, where all three were charged with being concerned in the robbery. Friend replied, "I got the horse from a man named Jack Lovell, of Bethnal Green, and the other two men only came with me for a rids." Riley said, "That is right." The other man made no reply. The horse was afterwards identified by Mrs. Munday, and the harness at North London Police
Court. Inside the cart was the square oil lamp produced, a collar, and the halter, which has been identified by Mrs. Munday. Gardner was discharged at the North London Police Court.
(Defence.)
JOHN FRIEND (prisoner, on oath). On Bank Holiday night (August 5) I was in Lea Bridge Road, accompanied by my brother and my sister-in-law, when I met a man named Lovell leading a harnessed horse with a rug over it. He said, "My trap has broken down up the road and I have left it. Do you know where I can borrow one?" I said, "No, I do not." Outside the "Shop Aground" I met Riley with Gardner. I said to Riley, "Do you know anybody that can lend a chap a trap." Riley said, "My landlord has got one. He will lend it to you for a bit of silver." Meanwhile we went to Nesbit Street, where Head lives, and dragged the trap round to the "Stag" public-house in Brooksby's Walk, where Lovell said he would meet me with the horse and harness. Lovell put the horse in the trap and away he went, saying to me, "Do you know anybody that will buy the old cob. I am a bit short of money and want to go to the market to-morrow." On the following morning I met him in Old Ford Road, and he asked me, "Did I know anybody who buy his horse?" I told him "Yes." He can said, "Here it is; do what you like with it. I want £10 down for it or you can have an exchange for another one, as I want some money." I got up into the trap, and going along the Old Ford Road I met Gardner and Riley. I said, "Halloa, would you like to go for a ride?" They said they did not mind and got up and accompanied me to Droking. When I got to Pixham Lane I left Riley and Gardner at the top of the lane while I went down to where I was going to sell the horse. I am perfectly innocent of having stolen the horse. I had seen Lovell on the Thursday before at Spitalfields Market with some bananas on his cart with a horse exactly like the one he gave me to sell, and I thought it was the same horse when I saw it one the Monday night.
Cross-examined. After I had sold the horse I intended to put the cart on the rail and send it up to London Bridge. I think I had 10 1/2 d. in my pocket. That would not have paid for sending back the cart, but I should have had money after I had sold the horse. It did not strike me to ask any questions about the harness or the horse because I had seen Lovell with it on the previous Thursday. He keeps a fruit stall somewhere in the Bethnal Green Road. When I was arrested at Dorking they asked me who this Lovell was, and I told the detective as far as lay in my power. I did not know Lovell very well, only through having seen him up and down. Lovell promised me a bit of gold if I sold the horse. I was to meet Lovell again after I had sold the cob at the corner of Brick Lane, Bethnal Green Road the same night. I have never seen Lovell since as, of course, I have been in custody, whereas if I had been out on bail I might have had a chance to look about for him.
To the Court. I did not cut the horse's tail; it is very saldom I carry a knife. I have known Riley five or six months.
HENRY RILEY (prisoner, on oath). I am a costermonger and live at 31, Nesbit Street, Homerton. On August 5 I was at the "Skip Aground," Lea Bridge Road, at about ten o'clock with a lad named Gardner. Friend came up and asked me if I knew where he could borrow a cart. I said, "Yes, I think so. My landlord has got use. and I have no doubt he will let you have that if you give him a bit of silver for the hiring of it." I went with Friend to Head's leaving the horse behind at "The Stag" in charge of another man Head was under the influence of drink, but I asked him if he would lend "Little Jack"—meaning Friend—his cart, and he said "You." Head said he would let him have the cart, and he took it away. I then parted company with Friend. Next morning I was again in company with Gardner in the Old Ford Road. We met Friend with a horse and cart. He pulled up and asked where we were bound for Being holiday time, we said we had nothing to do. Friend then said, "Would you like to go for a ride?" We thereupon got into she came and Friend drove us to Dorking. In Pixham Lane he asked us to get down while he was doing his business. We waited close upon an hour, and then Superintendent Boon arrested us and took us to the station, and charged us with stealing the pony. I did not steeling the pony, and know nothing about it or about the harness. Is in true that Mrs. Munday sold me some harness, and I dispossed of a to a friend named Smith, a greengrocer. I do not recognise the collar as the one which Mrs. Munday sold me. I think it was the second week in January last that I bought the harness.
Cross-examined. We drove from Old Ford Road to Epsom, and stayed there an hour, and I suppose it would be three o'clock when we reached Dorking. We pulled up several times, and, of course had a drink at each place. I had 4s. or 5s. when we started. Friend was supposed to meet a man at Epsom, but did not do so. We talked about various things over our beer, but it never struck me to the Friend what he was going to Dorking for; it did not concern no at all. I merely went for the ride, it being holiday time. I never considered how I was to get back, but I think Friend said he was going to exchange the horse. I am a fairish judge of a horse. The was a very aged old cob and showed a lot of wear. There was on old collar in the cart. It did not strike me to ask what it was doing there. I heard Mrs. Munday say she sold me that very celler, but that is a lie. I do not know where it came from or where the other harness came from. I heard Mrs. Munday say that I had taken a fancy to her cob on more than one occasion. That is false. I had never seen her cob in my life until I went for this ride with Friend and never wanted to buy it. I know Clapton pretty well. I suppose the "Ship Aground" is about a quarter of an hour's walk trass Reader's Field, where Mrs. Munday's horse was stolen from. Harvey's stable is close by.
HERBERT GARDNER . I am 18 and live at 58, Cross Street, Hackney. On August 5 I was at the "Ship Aground" in company with Riley. From came up with another man leading a harnessed horse, and asked if he could borrow a trap. (Witness gave further corroborative evidence.)
JAMES SMITH , greengrocer, Brunswick Street, Hackney. I know Biley, and bought some harness of him about nine months ago. I also bought the harness produced from Mrs. Munday. I think that was about nine months ago.
EDWARD DAWS , 173 Brick Lane, printer's cutter, also gave evidence as to the harness belonging to Harvey.
WILLIAM HEAD , 31, Nesbit Street, Homerton, carman, gave evidence as to lending the cart to Friend at Riley's request.
Verdict, both Guilty, and both confessed to previous convictions.
Detective-inspector SIMMONDS, D Division, stated that in 1901 Riley was sentenced to three years' penal servitude at the North London. Sessions for horse stealing. On January 22, 1894, at the same sessions he was sentenced to five years' penal servitude also for horse stealing, and there was a prior conviction for horse stealing on February 4, 1889. Altogether he had had 13 years' penal servitude for horse stealing, and was at the present time reporting to the police on his licence. For years he had been extensively engaged in stealing horses from Hackney Marshes and the surrounding districts, which he know intimately. With regard to Friend, the police had great difficulty in detecting a man of his class, as he was engaged in horse dealing. Witness had pulled him up taking horses along which had turned out to be correct horses, and Friend had then made a complaint to the Commissioner that he had been prevented from getting an honest living.
Detective-sergeant HAWKINS said that Friend had against him only one conviction of three months for horse stealing, with the exception of the conviction yesterday for stealing a mare and a gelding, but he was the associate of notorious horse thieves, one of whom was sentenced to four years' penal servitude and another to 18 months last Sessions. There was a great deal of this horse stealing going on, people turning their horses out to grass in the early part of the year, and the sufferers were generally poor people who could ill afford to lose their animals.
Judge Lumley Smith said he was satisfied that Riley was a confirmed horse stealer or dealer in stolen horses, and he could not pass a less sentence than Five years' penal servitude. Friend, Three years' penal servitude for each offence, to run concurrently.

DANSIE, George (40, plasterer) , stealing two china vases, the property of Edward Ager.
Mr. W. S. M. Knight prosecuted.
EDWARD AGER , builder, High Street, Plaistow. On Saturday, August 31, about 11 p.m., I went to see a neighbour a few doors away, leaving my door ajar. After a short time I returned and met charles Smith and a policeman; on going into my house I found that two vases, which were there when I left, had disappeared; their value was 5s. 6d. I have known prisoner several years and have employed him. At the police court he declared that he took those vases for wages due to him; I saw there were no wages due at all; I do not owe him a penny. I never authorised him to take the vases.
To Prisoner. You saw me during the week. I paid Ellis, the fishmonger, 3s. out of wages due to you; this was by your instructions.
CHARLES SMITH , labourer, 6, Albert Road, Plaistow. On August 31, about 11.20 p.m., I was sitting in my front garden when prisoner came and asked me if I wanted to buy a pair of vases; I said., "No"; he said, "come on, you can have them for a shilling." I asked him where he had pinched them; he said he got them from the fair at Wantead. He showed them to me; they ratiled, and I told him he would break them; so he turned them upside down. Three or four cards fell out; one had on the name and address of Mr. Ager, so I took the card to him. I did not take the vases; prisoner took them away.
Detective-sergeant FREDERICK STEVENS , Plaistow. On September 1 I went to prisoner in a public-house and told him that I was a police officer and should arrest him for stealing a pair of went from Ager's house the previous night. He said, "All right." I said, "I shall send you to the station while I go to your house." He said, "It's no use going round there, they're smashed; I would have smashed his b——head if I had met him." At the station he made no reply to the charge. I have not been able to find the vases.
PRISONER. What I said was that if I had met him I should have hit him on the head to the vases.
Prisoner's statement before the magistrate: "I took them for wages."
PRISONER (not on oath). I met Ager several times during the week and asked him for my wages; he said, "All right, old boy." On the Saturday I went to his house 20 times, and he was always out. I met Ellis, the fishmonger, who told me, "I have seen Ager, end he has given me 2s. out of your money, and he is going to give me the other 1s. by and bye"; I said, "What, give you 3s. out of 3s. 4d.; how am I going on about dinner to-morrow if I've only get 4d. to take?" I subpoeaned Ellis for to-day, but he refused to take the subpoens.
Verdict, Guilty. Prisoner confessed to a previous conviction, and his record was a very bad one. Sentence, 12 months' hard labour.
BEFORE THE COMMON SERJEANT.
(Thursday, September 19.)
[trialAccount t19070910-105]
WHITE, Henry (58, butcher) ; feloniously receiving one Ralli car, one gelding, one set of harness, and other articles, the goods of William McGinty, well knowing the same to have been stolen.
Mr. Sydney E. Williams prosecuted; Mr. Purcell defended.
WILLIAM MCGINTY , 718, Barking Road, Plaistow, laundryman on July 22 I visited my stables at Portland Road, and found them of correct. The next day the stable had been broken into and the golding, car, harness, and lamps stolen. On August 13 I saw the car at Minorles Police Station.
JOHN ERYCE , 417, Holloway Road. I have stable in Holloway Road. On Sunday, August 11, between three and four, prisoner drove up to my house with my boy. He had my horse in the trap, me boy having lent it him to trot out with the trap. Prisoner santed me to buy the trap for £16. I went for a run with it, and it seemed all right. I eventually agreed to give him £10 and my old trap for it, which I did on the Wednesday evening at six o'clock. Meanwhile on the next two days I went out with the trap, and on the second one I was stopped by a constable, with whom I went to the Minories Police Station, and was detained for six hours till my statement was investigated, on which I was released. On the next day, the 16th, prisoner came about 5.30 to receive the money. As he was signing the receipt Detective Eustace came in and arrested him.
Cross-examined. I had never seen prisoner before the Sunday that he called. Prisoner first called at my stables and borrowed the cob to drive the trap to my house. I am pretty well known in the neighbourhood, and sometimes buy traps in the course of business. After wanting £16 prisoner came down to £13 before accepting £10. My old trap was worth about £5. I told prisoner I had not the money at the time, and he agreed to wait till the Wednesday. He gave me his name as Henry Jones, 29, Halton Road, Islington. I arranged to pay prisoner in cash. When he had written the receipt, I said, "I will go outside and blot it." Whilst I was going out, prisoner said, "I am glad you are satisfied with the trap, and I will do anything for you in the future." I then handed the receipt to Detective Eustace, who asked who prisoner was. I told him, and he informed prisoner that he was a police officer, and understood that the trap had been stolen, asking him where he got it. Prisoner said he got it from a man outside a public-house in Holloway Road or Caledonian Road on August 9 for £13. The man was a stranger. The market in Caledonian Road to a well known place for buying horses. Prisoner said there had been a horse in the trap and he had taken it out and pulled the trap round to his stables.
Re-examined. Prisoner gave Eustace the address of 24, Halton Road.
Detective-sergeant EUSTACE. K Division. On August 14 I went to 417, Holloway Road, and saw prisoner in the front room with Mr. Bryce. I saw prisoner writing a receipt (produced). After telling him the charge, prisoner said, in answer to questions. "I bought it from a man on the 9th inst. He asked me £16 for it; I gave him £13. I was standing in the Holloway Road when I saw the man with it. He was driving it. He took the horse out of the cart and I pulled it round to my stable, a few yards from the address I have given you. I do not know the man I bought it from; I never saw him before; I haven't seen him since, and I have no idea where he may be seen. I do not know nobody that saw me buy it, and I cannot give you any further information." He gave his name as Henry Jones, 24, Halton Road, Islington. I took him to the station; subsequently to West Ham. He made no reply when charged I went to the address given; prisoner was not known there, and there was no stable near the house. I asked for White, as well as Jones.
Cross-examined. Halton Road would be about two miles from Caledonian Market. There is no mews in Halton Road. I went to No. 29 as well as 24.
Prisoner's statement at the police court: "All I have got to say is I appeal against the charge of having received the trap. I acknowledge I bought the trap and sold it to the gentleman. The other things I know nothing of."
(Defence.)
HENRY WHITE (prisoner, on oath). I was going up the market—we call it Smithfield—on Friday, August 9; I was standing outside the public-house in Holloway Road, near the railway arch, about quarter to one, when a man drove up in a horse and cart. He says, "Will you buy that lot?" I says, "No." "Buy the cart then." He asked £16; I said, "No, I will give you £10." He finally accepted £13. I said, "I have nowhere to put it; I have no horse; where can I put it?" He said, "There is a mews down here is Halton Road; go and put it down there and leave it." He directed me the way down and I went, took the trap, and left it there. I paid for the trap at the time I bought it. On the Sunday following I went to Mr. Bryce, having been told he wanted to buy a trap. I went to his stables first, asked his chap if it was true Mr. Bryce wanted a trap; he said, "Yes," and told me to take mine up to his guv'nor. He lent me the cob, and I went to Halton Road to fetch the trap, the boy coming with me. We then drove back to the stable, and from thence to Mr. Bryce's house. What passed between us was substantially what he had said; also on the Wednesday when the detective came. I generally sign receipts, "Henry Jones."
Cross-examined. There were hundred of people about when I bought the cart on August 9. I have not brought anyone who saw do it; I am a stranger about there. I had the money with which I bought the trap in gold in my pocket. I think the mews was at 29, Halton Road. I have nobody here from it. When I went to the mews to fetch the trap I left the boy in a public-house near while I fetched it. I live at Deptford. The detective never asked me where I lived; I gave the address where the trap was. I always buy things in the name of Jones. The money I bought the trap with was borrowed from my niece the day previous; I always get money off my niece. I do not live on her. Her husband is a green-grocer. I reckon I gave almost the full value for the trap. I do not ask questions when I buy in the market, and do not get receipts. I asked the boy if he would stop at the public-house while I fetched the trap; he said, "All right." He could have come if he liked.
To the Judge. They buy and sell anything at the Caldonian Market. I have no place to keep a horse or cart when I buy it. I sell a horse as quick as I can. Close to where I live there is a big forest where you can turn a horse out; they charge you nothing. I have turned out hundreds of them there.
Verdict, Guilty.
In 1873 prisoner received 12 months' hard labour for stealing; in 1874, at the Middlesex Sessions, seven years' penal servitude, stealing a horse; in 1880, eight years' penal servitude and five years' supervision; in 1888, six years' penal servitude; in 1893 seven years; in 1900 seven years.
Sentence, Five years' penal servitude.
[trialAccount t19070910-106]
DICKENSON, Edith ; feloniously marrying Charles Winter, her husband being then alive; making a false declaration for insertion in the register of marriage of the parish of Stepney; WINTER, Charles , aiding, counselling, and procuring Edith Dickenson to feloniously marry him, her husband being then alive.
Dickenson pleaded guilty.
ELIZABETH KATE KNIGHT , 13, Osborn Road, Southampton. I was present at Northam Parish Church on April 27, 1896, when the female prisoner was married to George Dickenson. I always knew her before that by the name of Mcivor , but that was not her proper name. She was married in the name of Twigdon , according to the register. I knew them for about two years after they were married.
Cross-examined. As far as I know they were living together. I did not see them after that for some years. I do not know Winter at all.
ELIZABETH WHITTAKER , 27, Nelson Street, Tidal Basin. I have known Edith Dickenson for four or five years. Twelve months ago she was living at 3, Francis Street, with Charles Winter, and had been while I had known them. I once saw Edith Dickenson and
Winter with the man Dickenson, in the "Freemasons' Tavern," Victoria Dock Road; that was a good way back, but during the time she lived with Winter. I knew Dickenson was her husband, because I have seen the marriage lines. He came to the house when Winter was not there. I had a room off Mrs. Dickenson in Francis Street. On the day the case first came on Mrs. Dickenson asked me to go and tell Winter to clear out, as he had no right there when her husband was at home. I never heard Winter mention Mrs. Dickenson's husband to her. I do not remember being asked at the police court if I had; I never knew anything about Winter and Mrs. Dickenson being married.
Cross-examined. Mrs. Dickenson always went by that name. I think she lived rather a loose sort of life, because she had the two men coming backward and forward. Both Winter and Dickenson were seamen. When they were both away she had the two half-pays. At those times I have seen men go in with her, but have not seen them sleep with her. It is 12 months since I left Mrs. Dickenson's place.
JULIA SHED . I was present on May 16, 1907, at Stepney Registry Office, when Edith Dickenson was married to Winter.
Cross-examined. Mrs. Dickenson declared to the registrar that she was a spinster; I always thought she was. I have heard about a husband named Dickenson. I had known Mrs. Dickenson for about eight months before the marriage. I knew Mr. Dickenson before the marriage, but not as Mrs. Dickenson's husband.
THOMAS GEORGE STACEY , Registry Office, Stepney, proved the marriage of the two prisoners, Winter and Dickenson, on May 16, 1907. (Copy register produced.)
SARAH DAVIS . I have known the two prisoners for four or five years as man and wife. I lived next door to them 2 1/2 years ago at Custom House. During that time I have seen the man Dickenson at the house. One day Mrs. Dickenson came to me and asked if I would let Charlie (Winter) stop with me as her husband was home. When Winter was in my house he said to me, "She has no business with her old man as her husband, as he knocks her about so." When Dickenson left Winter returned to Mrs. Dickenson. This happened during the six months before I left. Winter knew that Dickenson was married to the female prisoner.
Cross-examined. It was in my kitchen one Sunday evening that Winter spoke about Mrs. Dickenson's husband; there was another young man present. I turned round and said to Winter, "Oh, shut up, don't make any noise; I don't want murder in my house." He was rather upset about Dickenson being next door with his wife. I know Mrs. Dickenson used to get half-pay from her husband as well as from Winter, and the letter knew it too. I knew nothing bout the marriages.
Detective-sergeant GEORGE READ deposed to going to see Mrs. Dickenson on August 22.
(Evidence not admitted.)
Detective JOSEPH PAIN , K Division, deposed to arresting Winter, and telling him the charge, when he said, "All right." At the Station he said, "That's what she told me."
Winter statement before the magistrate: "I plead not guilty and reserve my defence."
Verdict, Guilty. Both prisoners were released on their own recognisances in £10 to come up for judgement if called upon.

BEFORE THE RECORDER.
(Tuesday, September 10.)
SURREY CASES.
WATSON, Sophia Annie, was found guilty last session (see page 621) of failing to notify to the police change of residence whilst a convict. (Prevention of Crimes Act.) It was now stated that, on the expiry of any sentence passed for this offence, prisoner would in ordinary course be sent back to the convict prison to complete or to serve such further part of her original term as the Home Secretary might direct. In these circumstances the Recorder passed a sentence of two days imprisonment.
BEFORE JUDGE LUMLEY SMITH.
(Wednesday, September 11.)
[trialAccount t19070910-110]
JOHNSON, Charles (22, engineer). and OWEN, James (21, labourer), pleaded guilty to burglary in the dwelling-house of John Cockburn and stealing therein 179 gold rings and other articles, his property.
Mr. Ricardo prosecuted.
Sentence, each prisoner, Two years' hard labour.
Mr. Ricardo asked his lordship if he would mention the conduct of the police.
Judge Lumley Smith objected that there were so many cases now in which an expression as to the conduct of the police was asked for. It was becoming a regular business, and he thought was a little overdone. The police were expected to do their duty, and of course in this case they were quite right. But he did not know that any extraordinary ability was required to stop two men at night carrying a bag on Richmond Green. He should think that was about what any policeman would do. He had been asked before that day to pass a commendation on the police, and it was becoming a little too common; but he always liked to encourage the police, and they had done very well on this occasion.
